        - So the only way `d8f6` "saves" the knight is if White captures it (`dxc5`), allowing me to capture the knight on e5 (`Qxe5`).
        - But White doesn't have to capture. White can just play `f4` or move the knight.
        - If White plays `f4`, the knight on c5 is still hanging.

        - Protecting it with a pawn? `b7b6`? No, `dxc5` still happens.
        - Protecting it with another piece? No pieces can reach c5.

    *   So, I must move the knight.
        - Options: `c5d7`, `c5a6`, `c5a4`, `c5d3`, `c5b3`.

        - `c5a6` and `c5a4` are poor.
        - `c5b3` is possible, but disrupts my harmony.
        - `c5d7` is the safest and most sensible move.

        - It blocks the bishop on c8.
        - But the knight was on c5, it didn't block the bishop (the bishop on c8 moves diagonally).
        - Wait, d7 is on the path of the bishop on c8? Yes, the bishop on c8 moves to d7, e6, f5, etc.
        - So `c5d7` blocks the bishop.
        - Is there any other move?
        - What about `c5b3`?
        - Let me re-examine `c5b3`.
        - White pawns: a2, b2, f2, g2, h2.
        - If knight is on b3, can any white piece take it?
        - a2 pawn: No.
        - b2 pawn: No (pawns capture diagonally).
        - Knight on b1: Can't reach b3.
        - Bishop on c1: Can't reach b3.
        - Queen on d1: Can't reach b3.
        - Bishop on f1: Can't reach b3.
        - King on g1: Can't reach b3.
        - Rooks: a1 and e1 cannot reach b3.
        - So `c5b3` is safe!
        - And `c5b3` attacks a1 (rook) and d2 (empty).
        - Actually, `c5b3` might be annoying for White. It puts a piece deep in their territory.
        - But it's a bit speculative.

    *   Let's re-evaluate `c5d7`. It's safe, solid, and keeps the piece. It's the move a professional would play to maintain stability.
